> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=Ugw9xrI_1wC2fxgJrYV4AaABAg.AM4MFEzIpEdAUPn7MlnSHW)  
>
> @thoraxepi This explanation mixes together a few different things. In Marxian terms, value isn’t created by perception or utility, but by the amount of socially necessary labour time required to produce something. People’s perceptions and demand can affect prices, but that’s not the same thing as explaining where value itself comes from.  
>   
> The ditch example actually fits Marx’s argument: if the socially normal way to dig a ditch is with modern machinery, then digging it with a spoon doesn’t create more value just because it’s harder, it’s simply inefficient labour. And wages aren’t determined by how “valuable” someone is either; they depend on the cost of reproducing labour-power and the balance of power between workers and employers. So the comment is basically describing how markets distribute income, not how value is produced in the first place.

> @julianyo7072 It’s very important to look at both sides of the issue. Do we even know for sure that gold would function effectively as a medium of exchange today? A gold-based system could lead to deflation, which might discourage spending and investment. In such an environment, businesses could struggle or even shut down because there would be less incentive for growth. The rigidity of tying money to a fixed asset like gold reduces flexibility in responding to changes in goods and services demand.  
>   
> We also need to consider how such a system would perform during a crisis. Central banks would lose a key tool, the ability to lower interest rates or expand the money supply, which could make economic downturns significantly worse.  
>   
> Finally, there are practical limitations to using gold in modern trade. Because it is a finite asset with relatively lower trading volume compared to instruments like U.S. Treasuries, large institutions could potentially manipulate its price more easily. In fact, gold markets are far less liquid, roughly an order of magnitude smaller, than the U.S. Treasury market, which raises concerns about stability and scalability.

When the student attempts to reiterate the point in a simpler way so that *Mr Jiang* can understand, he snaps back with 'You guys are stuck in the scarcity mindset. It's very convincing, so.. let's move on, okay? So this is something we'll go back to later on'. He never does go back to it. Last video he believed he was being victimized by 2 white men at a local swimming pool in Vancouver through the intimidation of their tall height, moral fortitude (to confront a stranger beating his son), and larger packages. This time it's the high school students who are ganging up on him.

> @ooga1484 I think you're right. There's a few true anecdotes, then some half-explained theories, then wild assertions based on treating those theories as facts.   
>   
> “Seeing a psychiatrist will make you worse” is an irresponsible blanket claim. Evidence supports psychotherapy and (when indicated) medication for depression/anxiety; exercise and social support help too. They aren’t mutually exclusive.  
>   
> “Crises and wars exist to destroy money so people keep working.” Is just a wild, unsupported conspiratorial claim. Economic contractions have identifiable mechanisms (credit cycles, policy shocks, terms-of-trade, etc.). Wars have complex geopolitical causes. Claiming they’re engineered to burn money is a wild claim and he backs it up with absolutely nothing.  
>   
> "School exists primarily to brainwash children into believing in the nation-state." An overgeneralization; schooling serves many functions; civic socialization is one, but it is also critical for networking and social capital, childcare and economic support, not to mention literacy. It's critical for the fabric of our society on a small scale as well, not just on the scale of nations.  
>   
> "Science is just a religion designed by scientists to trick you." Just a straw man argument.  
>   
> “Money is infinite; scarcity is a lie.” Just a lie. He hand-waved away the student's valid response. Nominal money can be created, but real constraints (labor, energy, land, materials, productive capacity) remain. Creating nominal balances without real output raises prices (inflation), not real abundance (Yes, regarding food scarcity there’s substantial waste and distribution failure, but global undernourishment involves poverty, conflict, infrastructure, climate shocks, policy).  
>   
> Here's what he's doing:  
>   
> - Discouraging evidence-based healthcare (telling depressed students professionals will make them worse).  
>   
> - Normalizing conspiratorial thinking about wars, crises, and poverty, which can crowd out workable policy conversations (food systems, social protection, monetary/fiscal choices).  
>   
> - Undermining trust in our institutions with “schools exist to brainwash,” while offering no constructive alternative other than teacher’s personal theory.  
>   
> - Fueling anti-science attitudes (think vaccine hesitancy), eroding trust in methods that actually correct error over time.  
>   
> - Encouraging magical-thinking policy (“just print more money”), ignoring inflation, real-resource constraints, and institutional checks.  
>   
> Don't be fooled. Conspiratorial ideas are rarely about the actual issues at hand, but about the narratives they promote. The stories that make you feel special and part of "the people aware of the truth" , and sometimes, spread an agenda. He offers no solutions, just the seduction of some sort of secret awareness. Question everything, yes. But that doesn't mean to just believe whatever you hear that's contrary to conventional beliefs. That's not critical thinking. It's performative skepticism “Just Asking Questions” to cast doubt while never staking a testable claim or accepting the burden of proof. He's adopting the posture of skepticism but only toward mainstream evidence, while credulously embracing counter-claims.

### **@JungleLearners** | 👍 15 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgzmuE1Xbf9cDnOjOCt4AaABAg)

🌿🦜Full Study Guide of this lecture: 
I created a study guide below for this lecture and will do the same for the second class next. While nothing can replace the professor’s lecture, organizing the notes helps me gain clarity. I did not add any of my own ideas—everything comes directly from the lecture. If something seems unclear, keep in mind it’s only the first class, so let’s be patient. Also, remember that the professor emphasized we are here to challenge the ideas presented (1:00:12). As he put it, this class is about how to think, not what to think (3:50). 

==================
COURSE OBJECTIVES: 

1. Understand The World: 
⭐Emmanuel Kant’s Perspective on Understanding the world is the following1:07: 
 ■  Noumena are things as they are, independent of our perception. We cannot directly know the Objective Reality of Noumena 1:33.
 ■  Phenomena are things as they appear in our minds. We use our senses to perceive the things of reality 2:02. Our perception alters the world into structures that we can process e.g. Time, Space, etc. 2:31. 
⭐Imagination: We will call this intermediate step in our mind a form of Imagination 3:22.       
⭐Class Objective: Enhance your Imagination to think for yourself, to understand the secret history of humanity and the theory of power underlying it 6:48.

2. Create Your World
 ■  By understanding the world now, and engaging Imagination, you position yourself to live a future the way you want to live and ⭐create a world that is true to you 7:21.
 ■  And, Collectively, you can contribute to the creation of a system that allows for Eudaemonia, the flourishing of your creative, human intellect 1:10:10.


=====================
COURSE METHODOLOGY:
 1. The Present: ⭐Create an Analytical Model that represents present-day geo-politics 4:55. 
 2. The Future: Apply the Analytical Model to make predictions about the future 5:25. 
 3. The Past: Apply the Analytical Model to the past to reveal the secret history of the world 6:03. 


===========================
🟢MONOTHEISTIC WORLDVIEW
 We will classify the geo-political system of the present as a Monotheistic Worldview where Science is the Authority acting as the dominant religion of the culture 40:56. Arising under the Monotheistic Worldview are three ideas that are transformed into tools of power. 
 1. The Individual
 2. Money
 3. The Nation-State 

The Individual:
Under Science, the Individual is a collection of memories and synapses. If we manipulate these properly, we can solve all our problems. We can control our well-being and command our fates 39:43. 
 ■  Humanity is understood at the level of Individuals independent of any joint community. 
 ■  ⭐The Individual is solely responsible for all personal outcomes, good or bad. 
 ■  Responsibility is Individual, and there is no direct responsibility by the broader community. 
 ■  Over-emphasis on this perspective makes individuals isolated and vulnerable to power applied against them 42:52.
 ■  Pleasure:
     ■ ⭐Happiness in this worldview is Individual Pleasure. 
     ■  Entertainment, Video games, Money, Friends, Love, Vacations. 
     ■  If you are unhappy, look to the authority of science, doctors. 

 
Money:
📈Money Creation: Or how Central Banking creates Power 7:38 
 ■  Merchants started banks to store their gold and facilitate trade with other merchants. The banks issued paper receipts for the deposited gold i.e. money that could be circulated independent of the gold 13:43.
 ■  The banks printed additional money, too, and they lent out that extra money to earn additional interest income.  
 ■  Now, with more paper money outstanding than gold deposits, bank runs became a problem 19:34  
 ■  To solve the problem of bank runs, banks organized into Partnership relationships to act as Cartels, allowing them to pool their gold to handle local bank runs. This is Central Banking 22:12. 
📉Money Destruction: Or how wars and financial crises reinforce a perspective of money scarcity. 
 ■  Kings need to borrow money from banks to facilitate war to control more territory. So the banks’ control over the capacity to create and lend that money, became a powerful force over the society. 
 ■  The wars destroy wealth reinforcing the perspective that money is scarce 28:42.
 ■  Central Banking also manages Financial Crises which destroy wealth and reinforce the perspective that money is scarce  27:26 
 ■  The perspective of Money Scarcity creates a powerful motivation for the Individual to work as hard as possible 29:01. 
 ■  Nuance: The real value is the work we do, not money itself. 

 
The Nation-State:
Before Mandatory-State-Schooling, professional education was facilitated through apprenticeship 52:46. Humans are born with the capacity to learn anything if given the chance 53:40. 
 ■  Component to the public school system is a transfer of authority from parent to teachers who represent the Nation-State 59:05. 
 ■  ⚔The first societies with mandatory public schooling were Sparta, the Aztecs, and Prussia (which developed the system we still use today) 55:48. These three were all war-making societies, dedicated to defeating neighbors. Their schools helped cultivate the society to support war for the Nation-State 57:04.
 ■  The Individual identifies not with the Community but with the Nation-State 1:03:01. 

 
🪄An Alchemical Pattern:
 ■  In the context of chemistry, alchemy was the pursuit to transform lead into gold. 
 ■  In the context of this course, alchemy is a metaphor for any pursuit to transform nothing into something, in particular to transform a concept into power. 
 ■  The aforementioned Implements of Power: The Individual, Money, and The Nation-State meet this classification.
 ■  We call it an Alchemical Pattern whenever a mechanism claims to produce something out of nothing. The pattern alerts us to make a closer investigation of what is actually happening 1:07:53.


=========================== 
🟢POLYTHEISTIC WORLDVIEW: 
Before the present Monotheistic Worldview, there was the Polytheistic Worldview. 

Fate:
 ■  Greek mythology explained that powerful gods affect day-to-day human life 37:08 leaving humans subject to Fate. 47:31
 ■  Fate controlled long term outcomes, so humans can only live for today, seize the day 48:55. 
⭐Eudaemonia: Happiness in this worldview is the state of Eudaemonia. Eudaemonia is living to the best of one’s ability within the confines of Fate. It is the flourishing of human intellect as creative people in the community. 


Collective Community: 
⭐Because humans lack ultimate control due to Fate, it is the Collective Community that affect change 42:52. 
 ■  As such, Humanity is understood at the level of the Community not the lone individual 32:49. 
 ■  Humans identify with each other, because they together comprise a community 42:52. 
 ■  Participation in the Collective Community mattered most in the form of generosity to others, e.g. a shared feast.
 ■  In this worldview, the worst punishment was not death, but exile from the community i.e. the ultimate alienation as an individual.

> **@nottwo** | 👍 2 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgxLrdH6zDtpCkyfxEN4AaABAg.AM4CXmpe4hfAMwbhd1iWTD)  
>
> @EzkNatfor the first question that would probably create run away inflation.  You’d be increasing incomes of everyone working on those development projects, and unless they all had incredible productivity payoffs you're likely not going to see enough consumer goods produced to keep up with the massive increase in demand.  This is why historically big wars are usually followed by really high inflation.     
>   
> As for the second question, sometimes that sort of happens.  The Fed influences the supply of money by buying government debt in the US.  When that debt matures or pays coupons the Fed usually refunds most of it back to the government.  But buying up all of the debt would increase the money supply too much and again lead to run away inflation.    
>   
> Hypothetically congress doesn't even really need to issue debt to pay for its spending.  It could just print money to finance its spending.  But issuing debt helps to combat the inflationary effects of government spending.  Increases in demand through the spending are offset by citizens choosing to save money through the offsetting debt being issued.

Money is a monetary expression that represents the energy and time invested in productive activities. When an infinite amount of money is printed, common sense suggests it loses value due to reduced scarcity and this is true. However, what's truly lost is the value of the time and energy originally exchanged for that money. In essence, printing limitless money devalues not just the currency, but the human effort it once embodied.
And yet I've only touched the tip of the iceberg.

"The real value isn't money - the real value is the work we do" incredibly on point statement - we often don't realize that the true value underpinning money - whether paper, gold, or even barter - is the human work that went into it. That's why printing too much money too fast leads to hyperinflation - there is too kuch currency and not enough human work to underpin it.

> **@turtlesoul**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgyHett1LMpYzBD5BCF4AaABAg.AMoKvS-nG2_AOwe0fMtExS)  
>
> Could you please elaborate/get more into detail on the last part? About inflation and hyperinflation? I've always had a difficult time understanding it... What causes this to happen? Why does the state print more money? Why does the value go down? Is it because human labor is worth less? Maybe I just answered my own question... Haha  

> **@adfluffy** | 👍 3 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgyHett1LMpYzBD5BCF4AaABAg.AMoKvS-nG2_AOwppFbffCx)  
>
> @turtlesoul  So inflation is a "natural" part of economies - in the United States, we have decided that around 2% annual inflation is healthy and able to be tolerated by the system. Hyperinflation is when the level of prices rise faster and faster, to the point where something priced at a dollar today might be worth two dollars tomorrow.   
>   
>   
> There are a lot of reasons that inflation occurs within and economy, so it's kind of hard to simplify down to a single reason. Hyperinflation, being so uncommon and extreme, is a little bit easier to pin down the causes. We usually see hyperinflation where governments print a lot of paper money for some reason (usually to pay back debts, but there are other reasons as well). A good example of hyperinflation to pay back debts is Germany following World War I - after the war, the ally powers required Germany to pay steep reparations, more than the Germany economy had the human labor to support. Since the paper money was no longer tied to the human labor required to back up the value of the money, the paper bills quickly became worthless. Once your economy enters hyperinflation it can be pretty hard to calm things back down, since the fear that prices tomorrow will be higher than prices today causes people to spend money as fast as they earn it so that it doesn't lose its value.   
>   
> The thing that is ultimately not taught regarding money is that human labor is the underpinning value of that money. Gold was only useful as a money when it was hard to find and required a lot of labor to extract. It still has those qualities today, though mining technology has improved our ability to extract it. But overall, the money in an economy needs to be backed up by humans doing work to produce some kind of value. Over time, as productivity (the amount of output one unit of human labor can create) goes up, more units of money are required to purchase things (generally). These are the basics of inflation - the changing relationship over time between the labor required to create and the prices of the things created.  
>   
> These are complex topics, and the examples that I give here are intended to give you an overview of the ideas, but as with a lot of this kind of stuff there is a lot of nuance that I left out. Economics is super interesting once you start looking at it as a study of how people organize and allocate resources rather than just a way to look at business. If you're interested in learning more, I'd recommend the podcast "Planet Money" as a good entry point - it's where I started before ultimately getting my Master's degree in the subject!

>   
> Intelligence determines how fast you can develop skills in abstract tasks.   
>   
> We can see this in his topic of doctor skills. Time + specific skill acquisition = competency.   
>   
> But intelligence acts as a multiplier of that formula. (Time+specific skill acquisition)*Intelligence=competency.

Really digs into how Abrahamic religions (Judaism, Christianity, Islam) abstracted God to enable all sorts of manipulative abstractions like infinite money and isolated individualism. But here's a pushback on the individualism angle, especially with Islam: while it does hammer home personal accountability (your deeds on Judgment Day are all yours, no excuses), it's hardcore collectivist in practice. Rituals like congregational prayers, Hajj, and Ramadan aren't just solo vibes—they're designed to weld the Ummah together, forcing community over ego. 

Some Reddit threads even nail it as "inherently individualist in accountability but collectivist in action," which vibes with how Islam channels monotheistic abstraction toward shared bonds, not isolation. And on economics—boom, it straight-up resists your "infinite money" illusion by mandating gold/silver standards (finite, tangible assets) and banning riba (usury), pushing wealth redistribution via Zakat for communal equity. Feels like your model oversimplifies the Abrahamic trio; monotheism sparks abstraction, sure, but Islam flips it into anti-elite tools that ground everything in shared, real-world resources. 

Thoughts? Would love a deep dive on how Islam fits (or disrupts) your psycho-history framework in a future vid! Keep dropping these bombs.

Dear Professor

Thanks for all your lectures they have been really interesting and have helped me a lot in my research.
I would like to briefly point out that yes money mass (as in finance is called M2) is in theory infinite and central banks can in theory provide or cancel money mass in or out of the system.
This however does not mean that we could provide money to the economy and save the system because what it is not infinite is everything else!  Every item, every good every service is finite by definition. For example the Central Bank could print trillions and give them to everybody and they could go shopping or spending them in the system, but the goods they would find in the market would be the same as before. Central banks cannot print more bread, more milk, more books ... you got the idea.
That's why in that case this creates inflation, precisely because the money supply is infinite but the amount of real goods produced are not and they still need a production chain to be delivered on mass.

This however does not go against your argument that the money mass is fictionally doubled so to speak, that part is absolutely true and if everybody would go to the banks to have their money back the banks would surely default, that's why police enforment always prevented that from 1929.

i think the argument for scarcity is unconvincing and the students are right to call it out. The underlying message is true and important. The powerful are who keep you poor because misery is something we want to avoid, so we'll work day in and day out to avoid it for us and our loved ones.

Again though the argument of money as infinite and abundance is unconvincing. We have an abundance of food *because* we have humans and human made things running around the clock to produce it. I do believe that we'd have enough food in a world where humans weren't threatened by misery to grow and harvest it. This said pointing out to waste the way he did misses the point that this is a cycle: the waste exist to engineer scarcity to force us to work *and* our work produce the abundance that can be used as waste to for us to work more.

The problem is specifically that the state of the world today is people being forced to keep producing more instead of producing enough.

> ​@kevalan1042yes, it's true, something that you are willing to pay for must have value. It all comes back to that. If everything were infinite, nothing would be worth anything, since it would be effortless to avail yourself of it. But the world's not infinite, so it makes sense that some things have to have a certain amount of value, and, when you have money, trading it between people is a good way to move around the objects of value that people want between each other. The problem I find is, when some people get a lot of money, they are allowed to pay to change the rules of the system and the playing field itself; lobbyists fund new taxes and regulations to harm competitors only, corruptioneers get exclusive preference that eludes competition, wealthy corporations pay for advertisement, which overpowers new players; the economy isn't a pure meritocracy, so it's not just about you producing value: you are impeded from succeeding by the existing conditions. If there are 5 massive companies already saturating the market, you are doomed to obscurity because they have already seized all the power: built all the infrastructure, made all the preparations and arrangements, pulled the right strings to get everything moving; they're prepared. Most importantly, they have impetus, momentum, mindshare - people know them, but not you. It's just truly really difficult in today's growingly monopolized world to succeed as a new guy. But can you make a small business that fills a niche that big business doesn't yet cover? Sure, I guess, for now. The trajectory seems to be that people will keep getting extracted from and outcompeted, until only big business is left. In this first place, this system is already global, yet no one upon reflection surely wants it to be this way. I'm curious to know what the teacher thinks is the solution to this debacle.

### **@MTrades_SMC** | 👍 57 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgzfCpMjgqPbgNXd8yl4AaABAg)

Great lecture. 

Some refinement on your ideas:
1. Question: Why don’t they print infinite money?
Because money has to be backed by demand, created through the illusion that it is valuable. The population’s labor (= demand) backs the money they print. If they print more than labor can support, inflation devalues that money.

2. Poverty
Poverty is what the powerful do to you. That’s true in a sense. Because in our reality, wealth is Pareto-distributed — it flows into the hands of the most powerful and is extracted from the weak simply because they can.

3. Crisis
A crisis isn’t necessarily money destruction; it is money redistribution. The money supply is still constrained by the demand created by people’s work. Those people save money and invest it into the redistribution machine called the stock market. A crisis simply transfers those savings into the hands of the powerful and prepared — often the very ones who orchestrated it. Yes, money supply is destroyed, but first it changes hands, usually from the orchestrator into the hands of the "weak".

4. War
War itself isn’t designed to create poverty but to funnel money into the hands of the powerful. Governments need excuses to pour money into the pockets of military contractors. These excuses are manufactured to drive popular demand for the redistribution of their own wealth. War also secures control over vital resources — oil, rare minerals, trade routes — all of which tie back into the same system of redistribution.

5. In the end
Everything is governed by supply and demand. But the powerful orchestrate the flows, manipulating crises, wars, and markets to extract from below while maintaining the illusion that money itself is valuable.

> You misunderstand natural selection. It isn’t “the strongest wins.” It’s the fittest survives—the one who adapts best to the environment. In our species, that environment is defined by hierarchies, leverage, and resource control. Fitness means knowing how to position yourself within those structures, how to exploit them, and how not to be seen as a threat until it’s too late.
  
> 
  
> Credentials don’t guarantee survival. Because they represent outdated theoretical knowledge, irrelevant skills, or strategies that no longer match the environment. Meanwhile, those who develop knowledge and skills that are actually in demand—and know how to apply them—will rise.   
>   
> Its all about the nuance of your thinking understanding all the variables the system operates on most people are blind to it relying on the beliefs they where brainwashed into thinking it would guarantee them a  job.   
>   
> While tools and markets evolve rapidly, human psychology does not. Those who understand how the minds of decision-makers operate rise faster, because they can adapt their moves to the real mechanics of power. Fitness is about understanding your environment—what it rewards, what it fears, what it needs—and shaping your skills to match that demand.
  
> 
  
> Money is no different than fire or steel once were: a tool that reshaped survival. It isn’t “unnatural,” it’s simply the terrain we live in now. Those who master it thrive. Those who don’t, perish.
  
> 
  
> Yes—power builds barriers. That has always been true. But barriers don’t negate selection; they are part of it.
  
> 
  
> Oppressed groups, minorities, anyone facing heavy disadvantages—this doesn’t erase natural selection. It intensifies it. Pressure creates collapse for most, but in rare cases it forges those who adapt so well they escape entirely. That is still selection.
  
> 
  
> As for me: I live by absolute responsibility. Whatever happens is the result of my actions compounded over time. Did I predict disaster? Did I prepare? Did I build buffers, acquire skills, and position myself so that when crisis hit, I had leverage instead of excuses? Poverty is not random fate—it’s the accumulation of failed preparation, weak positioning, or refusal to adapt. You are always playing the game, whether you acknowledge it or not.
  
> 
  
> So yes: poverty is the result of natural selection. It is what happens to those unable—or unwilling—to adapt to the demands of their environment.

> @ModernInformant The - poverty = natural selection/IQ/adaptation - framing is overly reductionist. Poverty is shaped less by individual cognitive horsepower and more by structural and circumstantial factors that precede IQ’s role. Where you are born (country, neighborhood) dictates access to healthcare, education, nutrition, networks, and opportunity, which set the baseline for later outcomes. Genetics affect not just IQ but temperament, impulse control, and emotional regulation, which strongly influence decision-making. And more predictive of success than IQ according to few research that I can site. Early experiences - trauma, nurturing, role models, parents - sculpt a person’s stress response and worldview. Once these conditions compound, IQ definitely matters at the margins, but the game is already tilted. Treating poverty as primarily a test of intelligence or adaptation ignores how much it’s about the starting line and the cumulative scaffolding around a person’s life.

Central Banks have a 'limit' to their money printing abilities, not because money is scarce, but because there exists a level where the 'illusion' of the value of money ceases to exist. Weimar Germany, Zimbabwe, there are many examples in history where the illusion of the 'value' of money broke. The entire system from education to law to media is designed to obfuscate this so that Central Banks can push the limits further. 

In essence, central banking then is the exercise of printing as much money as possible to finance projects for vested interests but not printing so much that it destroys the illusion of the value of money. Poverty exists because the existence of interest rates/usury which overall makes it so there is not enough 'money' in the system to repay the system's debts, therefore there will always be deadweight losses the system needs to allocate, which manifests itself as poverty

“The real value isn’t the money, it’s the work you do.” 🎯

Professor Jiang should’ve used cars as an example to explain illusion of scarcity. Companies don’t have to keep producing new models. They could simply just produce replaceable parts and keep previous models pristine. Instead we’re forced into a market that pushes you to buy new and dispose the old. You need money way more in an asset-based disposable economy than in a traditional one where EXPERIENCE is priority. It’s more of a philosophical question about managing human labor rather than speculating infinite growth. 

Every sector of our society has become a speculation machine. Ex. Polymarket, gambling, private equity firms etc. IMO we’re living in an era of aggregated autism.

### **@alma15093** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgwoURDhhs7h6KavHst4AaABAg)

Interesting lecture. One comment about Kant and reality. Kant did not say that reality is what we imagine it to be. What Kant argued is that the only reality we can know is that which is structured by our mind. This is very different from the idea that reality is what we imagine it to be. I understand that Dr. Jiang wants his audience to understand in ‘simple’ terms what the Kantian theory of reality is, but still. 

Yes, according to Kant, the world as we perceive it is the result of our senses and our understanding, and reality "in itself," independent of us, is unknowable. In other words, Kant set limits to knowledge, how we can know the world, arguing that our understanding can only know “phenomena” (what appears to us) and not “noumena” (the thing-in-itself). According to Kant, all knowledge is the result of combining data from sensory experience with the a priori structures of our mind, such as time, space, and the categories of the understanding, which he organizes in four main categories: Quantity, Quality, Relation (Causality, Community, etc.), Modality (Possibility/Impossibility, Existence/Non-existence, Necessity/Contingency). Consequently, for Kant, domains such as the existence of God, the infinity of the universe, or the immortality of the soul exceed the limits of scientific knowledge.

Just saying…

### **@sashxnok** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgwboprIOatFyAYJnwd4AaABAg)

Lecture was interesting to test my critical thinking. Based on an analysis of the lecture "Secret History #1: How Power Works," here is a critique of the teaching methods employed.
Overview of Teaching Style
The lecturer uses a deconstructionist and provocative pedagogical style. His primary goal is not just to transfer knowledge, but to "shock" students out of their current worldview. He employs a Socratic method mixed with a "Sage on the Stage" persona, positioning himself as the revealer of hidden truths ("Secret History").
Strengths of the Teaching Method
 * High Engagement & Active Learning:
   * Method: He refuses to monologue for long stretches. Instead, he constantly asks questions ("Why do we have poverty?", "What makes you happy?", "Why are we in school?").
   * Effect: This keeps the class alert. He validates student answers ("That's a really good point") even when he disagrees with the premise, which encourages participation.
 * Clear Conceptual Framework:
   * Method: He starts by clearly outlining the semester's roadmap (Past, Present, Future) and the specific goal: building an "analytical model" to predict the future.
   * Effect: This provides students with a mental "coat rack" to hang ideas on, making abstract concepts like Kantian philosophy or fractional reserve banking easier to follow.
 * Use of Thought Experiments:
   * Method: To explain complex concepts like fractional reserve banking, he uses a simplified role-play scenario ("I am a bank, you give me $5 million").
   * Effect: This makes abstract economic mechanisms concrete and understandable for a general audience.
 * Intellectual Provocation:
   * Method: He deliberately makes extreme statements ("History is a lie," "Scarcity is an illusion") to shatter students' complacency.
   * Effect: This effectively grabs attention and forces students to defend their assumptions, which is a key component of critical thinking training.
Criticisms & Weaknesses
 * Replacing One Dogma with Another:
   * Critique: While he claims the class is about "how to think" rather than "what to think," he often presents his own interpretations as absolute facts. For example, when a student suggests school is for learning or getting a degree, he dismisses it by saying, "The correct answer is brainwashing. Everything else is a lie."
   * Pedagogical Issue: This shuts down nuace. A true critical thinking class would explore how schools function as ideological state apparatuses alongside their role in skill transfer. By calling his view the "correct answer," he is ironically engaging in the same "brainwashing" or authoritative instruction he criticizes.
 * Reductionism and Dismissiveness:
   * Critique: He frequently oversimplifies complex issues to fit his narrative.
     * Example: A student raises a valid point about natural resource scarcity (food/oil) not being infinite. The lecturer dismisses this by pointing to food waste and calling scarcity a "mindset."
   * Pedagogical Issue: He dodges the hard counter-argument (that finite resources do exist regardless of distribution) to preserve his rhetorical flow. This teaches students to debate by deflection rather than rigorous analysis.
 * Conspiratorial Tone:
   * Critique: Phrases like "implanted into your brains by powerful people" and "central banking controls the world" lean heavily into conspiracy rhetoric.
   * Pedagogical Issue: While engaging, this can erode academic credibility. It frames the world in a binary "Us vs. Them" narrative (the enlightened students vs. the brainwashed masses), which is emotionally satisfying but intellectually lazy.
 * Pacing and Broad Generalizations:
   * Critique: In a single lecture, he jumps from Kantian philosophy to fractional reserve banking to the history of Sparta/Prussia to Monotheism.
   * Pedagogical Issue: The speed prevents depth. Connections are asserted rather than proven. For instance, linking the Aztec education system directly to Prussian schooling without context is a massive historical leap that requires more evidence than he provides.
Summary
The lecturer is a charismatic and effective rhetorical speaker who excels at capturing attention and challenging authority. However, as a educator, he risks stifling the very critical thinking he claims to promote. By framing his radical interpretations as the "only truth" and dismissing counter-evidence, he is training students to adopt his worldview rather than giving them the tools to independently evaluate all worldviews.

### **@monstercreed1955**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgyuR1Rdb8IJ8HCxVIh4AaABAg)

Hi @PredictiveHistory,

Thank you for your thought-provoking lecture and for encouraging us to see different perspectives.

Regarding your question — “Why not just print money and give it to everyone?” — I believe the issue is inflation. If governments print excessive money and distribute it freely, the value of money decreases. We have real examples like Venezuela, where hyperinflation caused basic goods like bread to cost millions. Printing money without increasing real production can destabilize the economy.

I also think money works largely because of collective belief. Society is structured in a way that makes money essential for survival — food, healthcare, housing, and clothing are all tied to it. This system conditions us to prioritize money above everything.

However, I wonder: since money is based on trust and belief, could society ever evolve toward a system where value is measured differently — not purely through currency, but through contribution and well-being?

I’m curious to hear your thoughts on this.

### **@pintospicks**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgxKqgn7OlIgXulwtVd4AaABAg)

Hoping prof Jiang sees this despite it being an old video. (Obviously my take is a factor, not the WHOLE reason we have poverty) We still have so much poverty because 95%+ of people dont care about money, they care about meeting their basic needs. People dont care to learn about money, how it works, what it is, where it comes from, how it can be leveraged and multiplied over time, people just care about having: 1. A purpose (usually their job, which people would do for free if they could have their needs met without money)
2. The basic survival needs (food water shelter companionship etc)

Since money has replaced god in modern society, it is nearly impossible to escape poverty (or participate in society) if you take 0 interest in what money is, or how money works.

Imagine you are a poor villager in the middle ages, and your society is centered around God (and not money). If you decided that you had no interest in god, you thought it was nonsense and that your time would be better spent on other things, you would be ostracized and unable to participate in society (or mobbed to death)

Likewise in modern society if you put no time whatsoever in the pursuit of understanding how money works you will forever be trapped in wage slavery

### **@ngocthanhpham1310**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgzBIedlku57DwbS4sp4AaABAg)

I disagree with Professor Jiang on what he said about money. He doesn't mention that the kind of money he's referring to is fiat money, and there are actually different types of money. Also, money is not the same as wealth — money is used to buy wealth.

One point he raised was about why governments don’t just print infinite amounts of fiat money. The truth is, they already have — and that’s what leads to inflation. When more money is printed without a corresponding increase in goods, the value of money drops. Simply put, there’s more money than goods, so the demand for fiat money decreases and its purchasing power declines.

### **@j0n**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=Ugx26swEt01VagWAbed4AaABAg)

This lecture presents itself as a framework for “how to think,” but the argument relies on rhetorical moves that collapse under basic scrutiny. It opens by invoking Kant to claim “there is no objective reality; reality is what we imagine.” That isn’t Kant’s conclusion. Kant argues we don’t access things-in-themselves directly because cognition structures experience, but he does not deny a mind-independent world. In fact, his project helps explain why shared, stable knowledge (like science) is possible. The lecture uses Kant as a license to treat reality as infinitely malleable, which becomes the foundation for later conspiracy-style claims.

The money section commits a category error by conflating “money creation” with “resource abundance.” It’s true that modern banking expands the money supply via credit, but money is a claim on resources, not the resources themselves. Expanding claims without expanding real goods and services produces inflation and instability. From “banks can create money” the lecture leaps to “scarcity is a lie” and then to “poverty is engineered to force people to work,” plus “wars and crises exist to destroy wealth so money feels scarce.” These are sweeping motive claims presented without mechanisms, testable predictions, or falsifiability. A theory that explains everything as intentional control explains nothing.

The lecture then contrasts polytheism with neuroscience/therapy and declares polytheism “more accurate.” But that’s a false binary. Recognizing structural forces, randomness, and power doesn’t require rejecting psychology or medicine; it requires a more nuanced view of how individual and social factors interact. The claim that psychiatry “will make you worse” is an irresponsible generalization that substitutes suspicion for evidence.

The schooling argument is the most plausible part—schools do socialize people into norms, credentials, and national identity—but the lecture turns a partial truth into an absolute: “everything else is a lie; the correct answer is brainwashing.” It cherry-picks a war-society origin story and ignores the multiple historical drivers of mass education (literacy, administration, economic change, childcare, social mobility), as well as the fact that families can indoctrinate too.

The biggest red flag is that disagreement is treated as proof you’re brainwashed. That’s not critical thinking; it’s immunizing a narrative against critique. If the goal is liberation, the method should be evidence, specificity, counterexamples, and clear standards for what would change the model—not a new totalizing story that can’t be wrong.

### **@MaxijCo**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgzJDcPsiXLo4g7glbh4AaABAg)

For a course that trains students to distrust authority, it asks for a remarkable amount of trust — specifically, that one teacher, in one classroom, has decoded what economists, historians, and scientists have collectively missed. It even enlists Kant for the cause, though rather selectively: a philosopher who spent his career establishing the universal conditions for objective knowledge becomes, here, a warrant for the idea that reality is whatever we imagine it to be. And for a framework that champions polytheism's unsentimental view of power, it treats the ancient world with a tenderness it never extends to the modern one.
The framework is more useful as an initial provocation—to shake up certainties—than as a finished explanatory system. The risk is that students, especially younger ones, will adopt it as absolute truth rather than as a starting point for thinking.

Printing infinite money and distributing it wouldn't generate infinite wealth because money is not inherently valuable, it's merely a representation of value. If every poor person was given 1million dollars, that wouldn't mean they are now rich, only that a water bottle that used to cost 1$ now costs 100$, the value of the water bottle remains the same even if it's price went up, that's what we call inflation. Value is determined marginaly, and it differs dramatically from circumstance to circumstance, we can generaly say that the value of a resourse, namely our water bottle, is determined by the relation between suply and demand, people being arbitrarily given more money does not affect suply and demand. The elites manipulate value by creating manufactured scarcity, which leads to a smaller suply in a targetted sector.

### **@free_thinker4958**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgyerkddJcg8hF2ktCx4AaABAg)

1. Professor, while military hegemony may sustain the "perceived value" of a currency, it cannot bypass the fundamental constraint of "resource scarcity." If the money supply expands without a corresponding increase in tangible commodities like grain or energy, does the currency not lose its utility? Does this not suggest that biological and physical realities—rather than political power—are the ultimate arbiters of value?

2. While the role of the "practitioner" is historically significant, the development of the microscope was a product of "theoretical scientific accumulation" rather than mere observation. In the absence of the Scientific Method, can "intuition" reliably address sub-microscopic phenomena like viral pathogens? Would a return to craft-based medicine, devoid of rigorous methodology, not risk regressing into the era of superstition and ineffective traditionalism?

3. If gravity is merely a human "interpretation," how do we account for its consistent effect on non-conscious matter and animals that lack human cognitive structures? Does this "universal consistency" not point to an objective external system independent of human perception? Is it perhaps a form of "anthropocentric hubris" to claim we construct reality through imagination, when empirical evidence suggests we are subjects to immutable cosmic laws?

4. While social structures certainly contribute to psychological distress, must we not acknowledge that the brain is a biological organ subject to physiological failure? If we would not treat a cardiovascular blockage with mere philosophy, is it not ethically problematic to dismiss chemical imbalances that cause debilitating symptoms? Is there not a risk of "theoretical cruelty" in denying patients clinical intervention solely to uphold a socio-political critique of authority?

### **@joaovitorreginattodasilva8840**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgwwK-NQS8f7kyBIPn14AaABAg)

His argument against the existence of scarcity is not as good as it may sound at first. "If food is scarce than why is there food waste?" Waste in a system refers to the failure to properly utilize a given resource, failure to utilize is not the same as over abundance, that's why surplus production is called that instead of excess. Excess is when you produce more than what you CAN utilize, surplus is when you produce more than what you WILL utilize, the difference is subtle yet immensely significant. 
Waste in the food sector specifically is primarily caused by failures or inadequacies in transportation, storage and distribution. These things are largely dependent on other resources of varying levels of scarcity (gas, electricity, labor etc...), and profit incentives present diminishing returns in terms of value as they go up, so actors in these fields would often prefer to have waste than to further optimise operations. 
The elites absolutely do create manufactured scarcity in some sectors to manipulate value, but from there to say that the concept of scarcity itself is a fabrication is crazy talk.

The section on two world views is a false dichotomy. It is not one or the other. Reinhold Niebuhr best captured our perception of reality in the portion of his prayer, as used by Alcoholics Anonymous, known as The Serenity Prayer: God, grant me the serenity to accept the things I cannot change, the courage to change the things I can, and the wisdom to know the difference. It acknowledges that there is that which is within our control and that which is not, and we need to be in relationship with both and determine the boundaries of our agency.  This is exactly the problem AI is having. Boundaries are the edges of our agency. When we respect each other's agency, we collectively do better.  That is all that human rights are about. There are boundaries to our free will.

### **@buzinaocara** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgxrGLgrOnqH79reNlR4AaABAg)

Major conceptual mistake by Jiang at 25:00 where he conflates money (currency, IOUs, etc) which is not scarce, with actual physical resources (food, raw material, land, energy) which are indeed scarce and limited by multiple factors. One can print money willy nilly, that that won't magically make more wheat or iron materialize. So printing money and distributing it would not necessarily solve poverty. His students actually seem to understand this better than him, and he shuns them for it. Merely printing money and giving it away could only solve poverty temporarily within a society of enough abundance, but even then that would be hard to maintain in a sustainable way without creating perverse incentive structures. The professor criticizes modern "brain-washed" money-centric thinking, while being a victim of it himself, by forgetting that a piece of paper does not intrinsically equate to an actual physical resource unless society organizes itself to make sure that happens. For him to think that simply printing money could end world hunger if only the elites were not greedy is terrifyingly naive and over-simplistic.

### **@romanjg1180**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgwASJ6DaLxAOtgjiSR4AaABAg)

Dear Jiang Xueqin:
I am interested in history and, above all, in understanding it. I have seen on your YouTube channel that you speak about it in a way that goes beyond mere data, offering an understanding of the "whys."
I have watched the first chapter of your Secret History collection, in which you argue that money can be created in an unlimited way. Although I believe the explanation is quite simplified, it could be accepted as valid; however, I find your conclusion that money is limited solely to keep people impoverished and motivated to work to be erroneous, or at least partial.
While this may be partially true, there is a major factor that you do not mention: inflation. If money is printed limitlessly in an economy with specific goods and services, it will lose its value. In extreme cases, it can reach the point where it is more useful for starting a winter bonfire than for purchasing goods or services, as happened in post-war Germany.
I believe this is a key reason why money cannot be printed indefinitely. You overlook this issue and focus entirely on social control. If this is one of the pillars of your subsequent arguments, I find it to be a weak one. I hope to continue watching your videos and learning interesting things, but as a starting point, this first episode leaves me a bit disappointed.
Finally, I would also like to mention that I missed any comment on the positive aspect of credit: it allows people with entrepreneurial projects to carry out their businesses and increase the wealth of society, something that would not be possible without it.
Thank you very much. I hope to continue finding interesting and reliable content in your future videos.
Saludos desde España.

30:28 he seems to fumble here when a student asks about land and oil being scarce. He just responds with "you guys are stuck in a scarcity mindset." He seems to think that every resource is infinite. He's right on fiat currency being virtually infinite. He's right that many things in today's world are abundant like food, but that's just due to advances in science and technology. 

There are still many things that are scarce like land, gold, and oil. Maybe some day scientific breakthroughs can make these things cheaper, but that would make the new scarcity, "good ideas." It's good ideas that make things cheaper and more efficient. And the people who can come up with the best ideas can charge a lot of money.

There will always be scarcity. It's just that what is scarce is always changing.

Hilarious, Kant is not the most important western philosopher. He's intensely derivative, and naive.

The description of finance is superficial and omits local grain based currency models. It completely ignores reality, and the byproduct of those systems, which was typically long term infrastructure. When your money represents grain stores your incentive is to spend it before it spoils, and that resulted in long term building projects. Metal based currency was for international exchange. There were many uses for money, because barter is cumbersome.

Is this course supposed to be meaningful? It seems like typical university trash, where someone says they're helping you "learn how to think, not what to think" and proceeds to misrepresented reality for gullible youth.

### **@trident0-q4m**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=Ugzejhj6wdojDDklk2h4AaABAg)

That was a horrible summary on Kant... He didn't mean 'nothing is real' or that 'truth is just constructed by power', Kant's point is that there is a reality independent of us, that we do not access it in an unmediated way, that human cognition structures experience. Don't use Kant as a launchpad to relativism. 

Also, banks create credit money, they cannot create real wealth. Money, and credit, help organize claim on real resources, real recourses are limited. Therefore, scarcity is not just magically abolished by monetary creation. Everything this 'professor' does is just taking basic knowledge, and turns it into heavily exaggerated, economically confused, philosophically sloppy, dangerous rhetoric. This is a deeply unserious explanation of how the world works, get out of this while you can.

### **@LogicalDelusions** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ajFXykT9Joo&lc=UgwLYma0jfWvBTDeu2V4AaABAg)

The banking example is so wrong. Banks create money in the system out of thin air. They do not lend the deposits they have. Indeed the money they lend is disproportionate to the cash deposits kept in the bank. Banks today barely have any cash and are not required to. When you apply for a mortgage, the bank types in the system that you now have a loan of 1 million dollars, and starts charging you amortized interest on that. So, the student who said the bank is not allowed to lend the whole $5 million is so wrong. The bank lends enormous amounts of money compared to the tiny fractions of reserve cash it keeps. In times of crises, people lose confidence and go to withdraw money from their bank accounts. This is when banks default and declare bankruptcy. This is also when central banks step in with a rescue plan to prevent an economical crash. No bank has the amount of money it lends to borrowers. Like I said, banks are not required to and barely have any cash in their safes. Bank safes are almost empty. What they keep is jewelery, gold, silver, diamond, etc. which people have deposited into safes.

This whole "modern society is fake and scarcity is a lie" take is peak pseudo-intellectualism. Its scary and cynically ignorant. 

1.​Scarcity is physics, not a conspiracy. You can print unlimited paper, but you can't print food or land—just ask Venezuela how "infinite money" actually works.

2.​Romanticizing the "ancient collective" is wild; back then, "collective" meant you were property of a Lord with zero human rights and died at 35.

3. In a purely collective society (like a tribe or feudal kingdom), you do not exist as a person; you exist as a function. If the tribe needs to sacrifice you to a volcano or the King wants your wife, you have no legal standing to say "No." Individualism gives you a legal shield against Power.

4.The irony of using the internet and literacy (products of the "evil system") to preach this is hilarious.

​It’s not a matrix, you just don't understand supply chains.

Throughout history people lived hand to mouth, food was hard earned, life was very very hard. People did not throw feasts because that was a path to happiness, it was because storing your wealth in someone else was a surer thing than burying it in the backyard. They don’t print infinite money because there has to be at least the illusion that it holds some sort of value. Do not disagree that the system is rigged and used against everyday people, hard disagree that just printing money would allow everyone to be rich. Beware reducing very complex ideas into simple answers.
